# Adi Shankaracharya (1983): the recited Sanskrit texts, subtitled

G. V. Iyer's *Adi Shankaracharya* was the first feature film made in Sanskrit, and much of it is chanted: Upaniṣads, the Bhagavad-gītā, Vedic mantras, and hymns attributed to Śaṅkara himself. The English subtitles you can find online mostly paraphrase these passages or skip them. Automatic captioning is no help either; Whisper, the best open speech-recognition model, turns chanted Sanskrit into near-nonsense.

So this repository does it by hand, or close to it. Each recitation in the film has been identified, written out from its canonical text, and translated into English. The tracks come in IAST transliteration, Devanagari, and English, timed to the film.

There are **191 subtitles covering about 70 texts**, from the Gāyatrī under the opening credits to the *pūrṇam adaḥ* chant that ends the film.

> **The film's own dialogue isn't here.** The conversations were written for the film and belong to its makers, so we have asked the National Film Development Corporation for permission before adding them. Until then, `tools/build.py --merge` can combine these subtitles with any English dialogue subtitles you already have, on your own machine. See [Watching with the dialogue](#watching-with-the-dialogue).

## Help check it

Some of this is certain and some of it isn't. The lines below are the ones we'd most like a second pair of ears (or eyes) on. "text" means only fragments are audible and we reconstructed the rest. "source" means the words are clear but we haven't confirmed where they come from. "timing" means the subtitle may appear too late or too early.

Click **flag** on any line to open a pre-filled correction. You don't need to know git, and you don't need to be certain.

The same flag links are on every line of [transcript/verses.md](transcript/verses.md), so anything else that looks off can be reported the same way. There are also a few recitations we couldn't identify at all, listed in [docs/UNIDENTIFIED.md](docs/UNIDENTIFIED.md). If you recognise one, please say.

## Downloads

| File | On screen | Good for |
|---|---|---|
| [`subtitles/combined/Adi.Shankaracharya.1983.sa-Latn+en.srt`](subtitles/combined/) | IAST above English | Most people. You can follow the sound of the Sanskrit and its meaning at once. |
| [`subtitles/combined/Adi.Shankaracharya.1983.sa-Deva+en.srt`](subtitles/combined/) | Devanagari above English | Devanagari readers |
| [`subtitles/Adi.Shankaracharya.1983.en.srt`](subtitles/) | English only | Just the meaning |
| [`subtitles/Adi.Shankaracharya.1983.sa-Latn.srt`](subtitles/) | IAST only | Following the Sanskrit closely |
| [`subtitles/Adi.Shankaracharya.1983.sa-Deva.srt`](subtitles/) | Devanagari only | Sanskrit readers |

The two combined tracks are also available as `.ass` files with the Sanskrit in a softer colour, for players that support styling (VLC, mpv). The `.srt` files work everywhere.

## Watching with the dialogue

These subtitles only appear while a classical text is being recited. If you have an English subtitle file for the dialogue that fits your copy, merge the two:

```bash
python tools/build.py --merge "path/to/your-english.srt"
```

That writes merged files to `merged/`. Your dialogue lines are kept, and wherever a verse is recited our exact text replaces the line that paraphrased it.

mpv can also show two tracks at once:

```bash
mpv movie.mp4 --sub-files="dialogue.srt;Adi.Shankaracharya.1983.sa-Latn+en.srt" --sid=1 --secondary-sid=2
```

(On Linux and macOS use `:` between the two files instead of `;`.)

## Which copy of the film these fit

The timings are for the full-length release that runs **2 h 39 m 54 s at 25 fps**. A quick check: right after the Chandāla scene in Kashi, the refrain *"caṇḍālo'stu sa tu dvijo'stu…"* should appear at **1:19:26**.

If your copy is a different length, or the subtitles slowly drift, [docs/RESYNC.md](docs/RESYNC.md) explains how to fix them. That covers a different frame rate, an offset, or one of the shorter cuts that circulate.

## Using the files

In VLC, use *Subtitle → Add Subtitle File…*, or rename the file to match your video (`MyCopy.en.srt` next to `MyCopy.mp4`) and it loads by itself. In Jellyfin, Plex or Kodi, put the file next to the movie and name it after the movie with a language code, e.g. `Adi Shankaracharya (1983).sa.srt`. For Devanagari you need a font that has it. Windows ships with Nirmala UI; elsewhere, install Noto Sans Devanagari.

## Every text in the film

Grouped by scene, in the order they appear. "solid" means we're confident in the text, the source and the timing. Anything else is marked with what to check. The line-by-line version, with all 191 subtitles, is in [transcript/verses.md](transcript/verses.md).

## Research notes

A few things turned up along the way that might interest people who know these texts.

- The sunrise chant at the start, and again at 0:53 and 1:36, is the hymn to Mitra, Ṛgveda 3.59.6 and 3.59.1. It isn't the Ṛgveda's wording, though. The film has *śravo… sānasim… satyam* where the Ṛgveda has *avo… sānasi… dyumnam*, and *prajānan* where it has *bruvāṇaḥ*. We think this is the Yajurveda tradition's version but haven't confirmed it. If you know, please tell us.
- The verse *ākāśāt patitaṃ toyaṃ yathā gacchati sāgaram* is the film's refrain. It comes back nine times, from Śivaguru's funeral to the last minute of the film. When it's recited it has the usual wording. When it's sung, the first line ends *sāgaraṃ pratigacchati*.
- As Śivaguru prepares to die (0:06:53), the chant is Bhagavad-gītā 8.12, 8.14 and 8.15, the verses on leaving the body while holding the breath at the crown of the head. Recognition produced gibberish here. Our first guess was 8.12 and 8.13, but running the audio through a voice-enhancing filter made it clear the film skips 8.13.
- Just before that, at Śivaguru's house (0:06:19), a chant we first took for more of Ṛgveda 1.90 turned out, with the cleaner audio, to be the Medhā Sūkta prayer *mayi medhāṃ mayi prajām* ("grant me wisdom, grant me offspring") followed by the Agni prayer *yat te agne tejas tenāhaṃ tejasvī bhūyāsam*.
- The Naciketas play (0:40) is staged as Kūḍiyāṭṭam, Kerala's Sanskrit theatre, and it runs through the Kaṭha Upaniṣad from 1.1.7 to 1.2.9 in the Upaniṣad's own words, with a couple of verses joined together.
- The Satyakāma Jābāla story (1:05) is first narrated by Śaṅkara from Chāndogya 4.4.1, then acted out in the Upaniṣad's words, condensed in places.
- The debate with the old Brahmin (1:25), who tradition says was Vyāsa, follows Śaṅkara's own commentary on Brahma Sūtra 3.1.1 very closely. The evidence it cites, the "Five Fires" of Chāndogya 5.4–5.9, is then chanted in full. In the third fire the recogniser heard *puruṣo vāva*, but the feminine *tasyāḥ* that follows only fits *pṛthivī vāva*, "the earth is a fire", which is what the Upaniṣad says.
- The lines Śaṅkara speaks after the Chandāla scene are the Kāśī Pañcakam verse *kāśyāṃ hi kāśate kāśī*. Our first draft had a guessed reconstruction here, and a clearer pass showed it was a real verse.
- On his travels the film has Śaṅkara pass other traditions: the Buddhist Three Refuges at dawn (0:04:17) and the Jain Navkār mantra on the road north (0:46:17).
- The last texts, on the climb into the Himalayas, are Bhagavad-gītā 18.51, 18.49 and 18.53 strung together, and the film ends on *pūrṇam adaḥ pūrṇam idam*.
- Small amounts of Malayalam, Hindi and Kannada or Tulu are spoken or sung too (listed in [UNIDENTIFIED.md](docs/UNIDENTIFIED.md)).

## How it was made

Whisper large-v3 transcribed the soundtrack three times. One of those passes went back over the stretches the first one skipped, because Whisper's voice detector throws away chanting over music, which is exactly what we needed. The output was treated as clues rather than text. The identification and translation were done by AI (Claude), working from the surviving syllables, the metre, the grammar and the canonical texts, and then reviewed by a person. The English is new, translated from the Sanskrit rather than adapted from existing subtitles.

The whole process, including what went wrong, is in [docs/METHODOLOGY.md](docs/METHODOLOGY.md).

## The master transcript

Everything is generated from one file, [transcript/transcript.txt](transcript/transcript.txt), which holds each recited text with its timing, Devanagari, English, source, confidence and notes. `transcript.json` and `transcript.tsv` are machine-readable copies.

```bash
pip install -r requirements.txt
python tools/build.py                       # rebuild everything after an edit
python tools/build.py --fps 25:23.976       # a set for a copy that runs at 23.976 fps
python tools/build.py --anchor 00:18:37.4=00:18:31.0 --anchor 01:19:26.4=01:17:02.7
                                            # fit to your copy from two matching moments
```

The file format is in [docs/TRANSCRIPT_FORMAT.md](docs/TRANSCRIPT_FORMAT.md).

## Contributing

The easiest way is a **flag** link, or [opening a correction](../../issues/new?template=correction.yml) directly. Pull requests that edit the transcript are welcome too; [CONTRIBUTING.md](CONTRIBUTING.md) has the details.
